THE INK OF FORGETTING

Chapter 1

CHAPTER 1

Cairo’s dust tasted of rust and forgotten sugar. It coated the back of his throat, this grit that was once a palace, a market, a man. He walked through a city that remembered itself differently, its avenues now sketched in phantom lines across his tongue.

It was not a grand theft. Not a whole, vaulted dome of it. Just a piece, a ragged swatch the colour of a day-old bruise, torn from the horizon where the sea met the nothing. I stole it with my eyes, then with the stub of charcoal I kept in my pocket, smearing it onto the blank ration-book page. The act was quiet, desperate. A colonial child’s magic: if I could take the sky into my hands, then perhaps the sky, and the world it roofed, did not yet own me completely.

“Elias.”

My father’s voice was a stone dropped into the shallow well of my concentration. I did not look up. I was adding the suggestion of the old fort on the headland, a crumbling molar against the gumline of the land.

“Elias. The light is going.”

It was. The stolen bruise was deepening into a venous purple. The air, thick and warm as blood, began to cool at its edges. I closed the ration book, the sky trapped between its pages, and stood from the rock where I’d perched. My knees popped. Sixteen years old and my body already sounded like an old house settling.

He was waiting on the path, a silhouette leaning on his carved stick. Not for support—his legs were still good—but as a pointer, a conductor’s baton for a world that refused to keep time. Professor Alistair Finch, formerly of King’s College, presently of Nowhere, the Uncharted Hinterland of His Own Mind. He wore a linen suit the colour of dust, and the dust here loved him for it, clung to him like a second, more honest skin.

“You were drawing the fort,” he said, not a question. He had the unsettling habit of knowing what you were doing without looking.

“I was drawing the absence of the fort,” I corrected, falling into step beside him. Our dialogue was a worn ritual. “The shape its collapse makes in the air.”

“Sophistry,” he said, but I heard the faint, dry click of approval in his throat. “The empire built in stone. We critique in ghosts. A fair trade, I suppose.”

The path wound down from the bluffs into the town—our town, though we were not of it. Port James was a fever-dream the British Empire had sweated out onto the coast a century ago. A colonial administration building with a wedding-cake facade, now housing a lethargic post office and a family of bats. A cricket pitch gone to meadow, the pitch itself a stubborn, bald rectangle where the ghosts of dead sportsmen probably still argued over LBW decisions. Shops with names like “The Regal Mercantile” selling fishing nets and paraffin. And everywhere, the people who had been here before the names, moving through the English geometry with a patient, tectonic disregard, waiting for the geometry to forget itself.

Which it was doing. Gloriously.

We lived in the old District Officer’s bungalow, a white elephant of a house with a wide veranda that groaned under the weight of its own nostalgia. My father had been allowed to keep it—a courtesy, or a joke. The new ministry man, Mr. Cheltham, lived in a modern concrete box near the harbour. Our house was a museum, and we were its last, confused curators.

Mother was on the veranda, shelling peas into a chipped porcelain bowl that bore the faded crest of some forgotten railway company. Her movements were precise, surgical. Each pod gave a soft snick, a green whisper of execution. She was a Parisian painting that had somehow been hung in this humid, salt-rotted gallery. Fine bones, eyes the grey of a winter Seine, hair perpetually escaping its knot in elegant, weary tendrils. She had followed my father here for “fieldwork” twenty years ago. The field had consumed the work, then the ambition, and now it was patiently consuming them both.

“The boy was communing with ruins again,” Father announced, lowering himself into a cane chair that sighed in protest.

“He gets it from you,” she said, not looking up. Her French accent was a delicate scar on her English. “A fascination with empty things.”

“Nothing is empty, Claire. Only waiting for a better story.”

I slipped inside, away from the theatre of their quiet war. The house smelled of mildew, old paper, and the ghost of kerosene. My room was at the back, overlooking what had been an English garden and was now a glorious, tangled riot of local flora winning back its territory. I placed the ration book on my desk, opening it to the stolen sky. In the dim light, it looked less like a sky and more like a stain. A Rorschach blot. What do you see? I see a country that wasn’t mine, forgetting the shape it was told to be.

Dinner was the peas, a piece of fried fish tasting vaguely of diesel, and silence broken by the distant, rhythmic thump of generators from the port. The new sound. The future’s heartbeat.

“Cheltham stopped by today,” Mother said, finally. The words lay on the table like a warrant.

Father chewed slowly. “And?”

“He was… assessing. The house. The land. He mentioned the ‘underutilized potential’ of the officer’s quarters. Said the ministry is considering ‘repatriation of colonial assets to the people’s utility.’”

Father put his fork down. It clinked against the plate, a tiny, significant gong. “He wants to turn it into a clinic. Or a school. Something worthy.”

“It might be,” she said softly, aiming the words not at him, but at the space between them.

“This house is a document,” Father said, his voice rising into the register he used for lectures to empty chairs. “You don’t turn a document into a clinic. You read it. You learn why it was written in the first place.”

“It was written because we had the pens, Alistair. And the paper. And the arrogance.” She looked at him, finally. “Now someone else has the pen. They will write a clinic. Or a school. It is what people need.”

“What people need is to understand the architecture of their prison before they paint the walls!”

I stared at my fish. This was the familiar fault line. He lived in the grand, tragic narrative of Empire’s End. She lived in the practical, desperate reality of What Comes Next. I lived in the crack between them, drawing pictures.

The argument subsided, not with a resolution, but with exhaustion. It was a tide that went out, leaving the bleak, familiar debris on the shore. After helping Mother clear, I went back to the veranda. The night was absolute, a velvet negation. The stars here were not polite, distant pinpricks. They were a furious, milky spill, a cosmic accident. I could hear the sea chewing on the shore.

And then, a new sound. Not the generators. A low, rhythmic, wooden clack-clack-clack.

I peered into the darkness. Down by the old boathouse, a shadow moved against the greater shadow. A figure, bending, straightening. Clack-clack-clack. It was Old Joseph, mending his nets by lantern light. He was of the people who were here before the names. He taught me how to track a storm in the taste of the air, how to see the fish beneath the water’s skin. He never called me “master” or “boy.” Just Elias. In his language, it meant “the one who watches.”

I slipped off the veranda, my feet finding the packed earth path by memory. The air was cooler near the water, smelling of salt and rotting wood. Joseph didn’t look up as I approached. His hands, a map of scars and knots, worked the needle and twine with an economy that seemed holy.

“You are out late, Watcher,” he said in his deep, gravelly English.

“Couldn’t sleep.”

“The house is arguing with itself again.”

“Yes.”

Clack-clack-clack. He finished a knot, bit the twine. “The new man. Cheltham. He asks many questions about the old places. The fort. The caves to the north. The mission hospital on the ridge.”

“He’s an administrator. He likes to know what he’s administering.”

Joseph gave a soft grunt that was not quite agreement. He looked at me, his eyes catching the lantern light, two chips of obsidian. “Some things do not wish to be administered, Elias. Some things are… finished. But not gone. They are waiting.”

“For what?”

“For a better story,” he said, echoing my father with a twist that made the phrase entirely his own. He pointed his needle toward the black mass of the headland. “Your fort. The soldiers are gone. The guns are rust. But the stone is not quiet. At night, it dreams of the weight it once carried. Dreams are heavy. They can pull other things into them.”

It was the kind of thing my father would say, but from Joseph it wasn’t philosophy. It was a weather report. A warning.

“What kind of things?”

He shrugged, a massive movement of his shoulders. “Things that are lost. Things that are between. The world is not a clean page, Watcher. It is a palimpsest. Many writings, one over the other. Sometimes the old writing shows through.” He returned to his net. “Do not go drawing up there at night. The dreams there are not for your little book.”

I walked back to the house, his words clinging to me like cobwebs. The bungalow was dark except for a single light in my father’s study. I saw his silhouette, bent over his desk, probably annotating some map of a country that no longer existed. A man tending the grave of an idea.

In my room, I opened the ration book again. I looked at my stolen sky, my crumbling fort. Then I turned to a fresh page. I didn’t think. I just let my hand move. It wasn’t the fort. It wasn’t the sky. It was a shape that came from between them. A dark, vertical slit in the world. A doorway, but one that was closed, or perhaps just waiting to be opened. Around it, I drew the faint, swirling suggestions of things being pulled toward it. Not with lines of force, but with lines of longing.

It was the best drawing I’d ever done. It was also the most terrifying.

I closed the book quickly, as if I could shut the door I’d just imagined. I lay in bed, listening to the house breathe. The clack-clack-clack of Joseph’s net-mending was gone. In its place was a deeper silence, the kind that comes after a question has been asked but before the answer arrives.

That night, I dreamed of stones dreaming. And in their dreams, I was small, and I was walking toward a door that was not a door, and from behind it came a sound like pages turning, and a smell like rain on hot dust, and the feeling of being watched by something that had no eyes.

Chapter 2

CHAPTER 2

The ministry car was a black beetle, polished to a predatory shine, sucking up the dust of our driveway. It did not belong. Its very geometry was an insult to the soft, decaying edges of Port James.

Mr. Roland Cheltham emerged like the car’s secreted soul. Mid-forties, hair the colour of wet sand and cut with mathematical severity. He wore a short-sleeved white shirt, crisp tan trousers, and an expression of benign efficiency. He carried a clipboard.

“Professor Finch! A pleasure to catch you at home.” His voice was a calibrated instrument, pitched to convey approachable authority.

My father stood on the veranda, a king in a bathrobe holding a cup of tepid tea. He did not smile. “Mr. Cheltham. To what do we owe the incursion?”

Cheltham’s smile was a hyphen. “Just a follow-up, Professor. A friendly chat about the future.” His eyes swept the veranda, the peeling columns, the bougainvillaea that was staging a hostile takeover of the east wall. They took inventory. “May I?”

He was already mounting the steps. He nodded at my mother, who stood in the doorway, a dishcloth in her hands like a sceptre. “Madame Finch.”

“Monsieur Cheltham.”

He took a seat without being invited, placing the clipboard on the wicker table. I lingered in the shadows of the doorway, a ghost in my own home.

“It’s about potential, Professor,” Cheltham began, lacing his fingers. “This district has tremendous potential. But potential is like a battery—it must be directed, earched, to do any good. The old structures,” he gestured vaguely at the house, “while historically fascinating, represent a… a static charge. A memory of current, not current itself.”

My father sipped his tea. “You want to turn my home into a wire.”

“Into a conduit,” Cheltham corrected, pleased with the word. “For progress. For the people. The ministry is drafting a comprehensive Land Re-alignment Act. This property, given its size and location, is ideal for a vocational training centre. Masonry, mechanics. Tangible skills.”

“And where shall I do my work?” Father’s voice was dangerously quiet. “My study of colonial administrative decay? Shall I conduct it from a cot in the masonry shed?”

“Your work,” Cheltham said, leaning forward slightly, “is, if you’ll pardon me, a rear-view mirror. The nation is driving forward. We need to look through the windshield.” He tapped his clipboard. “We’re offering a generous compensation package. And assistance with repatriation.”

The word hung in the air. Repatriation. To where? England was a story he told, a series of grey photographs in a damp album. He was no more English now than the geckos on the wall.

“This is my home,” Father said, the simple sentence a fortress.

“It was the District Officer’s home,” Cheltham replied gently. “A role which, with all respect, no longer exists. We are all citizens of the new republic now. Equal. We must all contribute to its body.” He stood, his mission accomplished. “Think on it. The proposal is detailed here.” He slid a typed document from his clipboard. It lay on the table like a subpoena. “No immediate rush. But the future, as they say, waits for no man.”

He left with a final, inclusive smile that took in my mother, the house, and the invisible me in the doorway. The beetle reversed, crunching gravel, and slithered back toward the concrete future.

Father stared at the document. He did not touch it. Mother walked over, picked it up, and began to read. Her face showed nothing.

“They are not wrong, Alistair,” she said after a moment. “A training centre. It is a good thing.”

“It is a bland thing!” he exploded, finally. “It is the replacement of a complex, tragic history with a… a manual! They want to turn a library into an instruction booklet for plumbing!”

“People need plumbing more than they need your tragedy!”

“Without the tragedy, the plumbing is just pipes in the dirt! It has no meaning!”

“Meaning does not fill a child’s belly!”

I left them to it. The air in the house was too thick to breathe. I grabbed my ration book and charcoal and fled.

I walked north, away from the town, along the coastal path. The sun was a hammer on the anvil of the sea. I needed to draw something clean, something simple. A rock. A single, defiant tree. I climbed the headland, not toward the fort, but to a smaller promontory where a lone, twisted casuarina stood, its roots clawing into the stone like desperate fingers.

I sat, opened the book. But the clean line wouldn’t come. My fingers felt Cheltham’s presence, his clipboard certainty, smudging the air. I saw not the tree, but the potential of the tree. Good for firewood. For timber. A thing to be assessed, utilized.

Frustrated, I stood and walked further, almost without thinking, toward the old fort. Joseph’s warning whispered in my head, but the daylight scorned it. The fort was just a ruin. A collection of stones with a bad history. What could it do?

The British had built Fort Gregory to command the harbour, a statement in limestone and cannon. The statement had faded. The cannons were gone, sold for scrap or swallowed by the sea. The walls were tattooed with lichen and the vulgar poetry of decades of lovers and vandals. The main gate was a gaping mouth with broken teeth.

I stepped inside. The courtyard was a bowl of sunlight and silence. Weeds grew between the flagstones. In one corner, a frangipani tree had burst through the stone, its sweet, funereal scent at odds with the martial geometry. This was what my father loved: the victory of the incidental over the intentional.

I found a shaded alcove, the remains of a barracks room, and sat. I would draw the frangipani. A life that had broken a fortress. That was a story.

I was sketching the first limb when I heard it. A scrape. Not an animal scuffle. A deliberate, dry drag of stone on stone.

I froze, my charcoal hovering.

It came from deeper within the fort, from the doorway that led to the old magazine stores, the underground rooms. That doorway was always dark. A black rectangle.

The scrape came again. Then a sigh. Not a human sigh. A release of air from a deep, confined place.

Every cell in my body told me to run. But my father’s blood in me, the blood that communed with ruins, held me fast. Observe. Document.

I stood, silent, and edged toward the barracks doorway, peering across the sunlit courtyard toward the dark one.

A figure emerged.

Not Joseph. Not a villager. It was a man, but wrong. He was dressed in a British officer’s tropical uniform, but the khaki was bleached to the colour of bone, the fabric hanging loose as if on a skeleton. His face was in shadow under his peaked cap, but I could see the gaunt line of his jaw. He did not walk. He shuffled, his boots scraping on the flagstones. In his hands, he held not a weapon, but a ledger, clutched to his chest.

He moved to the centre of the courtyard and stopped. He looked up, not at the sun, but at the sky as if reading a memo. He opened the ledger. His lips moved, but no sound came out. He was counting. Taking inventory of nothing.

A cold that had nothing to do with shade poured through me. This was not a ghost story. This was worse. It was a bureaucracy of a ghost. A stuck record of administration.

He turned his head, slowly, and I swear the shadows where his eyes should be rested on me.

He did not startle. He did not threaten. He simply closed his ledger, gave a small, officious nod—subject observed, logged—and shuffled back into the dark doorway.

The scrape of his boots faded.

I ran. I ran so fast I tore the page of the frangipani sketch. I didn’t stop until I was back on the main path, the sun hot on my neck, the sane, diesel-scented air of the port in my lungs.

I sat on a rock, trembling. Had I seen it? Had the heat and the tension with Cheltham and my father cooked a vision in my brain?

I opened my ration book, to a fresh page. My hand was shaking. I didn’t draw the officer. I drew the doorway. The dark rectangle. And from it, I drew lines, not of longing this time, but of accounting. Neat, columns of nothing, pouring out into the world.

When I got home, the house was silent. The document from Cheltham was still on the table. My father was in his study, the door closed. My mother was in the garden, viciously deadheading flowers.

I went to my room and lay down. I could still hear the scrape. The dry, official scrape of a world that was finished but refused to file itself away.

That night at dinner, my father was eerily calm. He spread butter on his bread with meticulous care.

“I have decided,” he announced, “to mount an expedition.”

Mother paused, her fork halfway to her mouth. “An expedition.”

“To the interior. To the old mission hospital on Ridgeback Hill. Cheltham mentioned it. It’s the next ‘asset’ on his list. I intend to document it fully—architecturally, historically—before it becomes a… a community latrine.” He said the last words with distilled contempt.

“The mission hospital is a day’s hard walk, Alistair. It is abandoned. It is dangerous. The roof—”

“All the more reason to go. To capture it before it is captured by the philistines.” He looked at me. “Elias will come. He can draw. A proper record.”

My blood went cold. The interior. The hills. The places Joseph had said were “finished but not gone.”

“I… I have school,” I stammered, a pathetic defence.

“You have a better education waiting in those hills. We leave Friday.”

Mother stared at him. She did not argue. She just looked at him as if he were a sentence in a language she had finally decided to stop trying to translate.

Later, on the veranda, I found her smoking one of her rare cigarettes. She offered me one. I took it. The smoke was harsh, foreign.

“He is building a coffin,” she said, her voice flat. “And he is using the last of our wood to do it. This expedition. It is not about documentation. It is a protest. A performance of his own relevance.” She looked at me, her grey eyes full of a terrible, clear sadness. “Do not become a prop in his play, Elias. Your drawings… they are your own. Do not let him write the captions for them.”

I thought of the ledger-ghost in the fort. The official record of emptiness. Was that what my father was becoming? A man clutching the ledger of a dead empire?

And what was I? The boy who drew the doorways things came out of.

I finished the cigarette, the ember a tiny, fierce eye in the dark. Friday was two days away. Two days until we walked into the hills, toward the next ruin on Cheltham’s list. Toward the things that were waiting, between the writings, for a better story.

Or perhaps, for a final one.

Chapter 3

CHAPTER 3

We left at dawn, under a sky the colour of cold ash. Father carried a leather satchel bulging with notebooks, a brass compass, and a canteen. I had a small pack with water, a little food, and my ration book, now dangerously close to full. He had dressed for a Victorian exploration: sturdy boots, khaki trousers tucked into socks, a wide-brimmed hat. I wore what I always wore.

Mother stood on the veranda, arms crossed. She did not wave. She was a statue titled Resignation.

The path out of Port James quickly turned from packed earth to root-tangled track, climbing through plantations of rubber trees that stood in silent, regimented rows, their trunks scarred with old tap-cuts like tribal markings. They were a ghost economy, these trees. Another forgotten intention. The air under the canopy was cool and smelled of damp rot and memory.

Father set a brisk pace, his stick punching the ground. “The mission was established in 1887,” he began, his lecture voice finding its rhythm in the rhythm of his steps. “A joint venture of the Church and the colonial office. A beacon of civilization and medicine. Of course, it was also a lever. Convert the body, you gain a foothold with the soul. Convert the soul, and the land follows.”

I walked behind him, watching the way his shoulders hunched slightly, as if still carrying the weight of the house’s high ceilings. “Did it work?”

“For a time. Then the fever years came. Malaria, dengue. The missionaries died almost as fast as their patients. It became a hospice, then a storage depot, then a ruin. A perfect metaphor for the entire project: a house of healing that succumbed to the very climate it sought to conquer.”

He spoke of it with a perverse pride, as if its failure made it more precious. A successful mission would have been boring. A tragedy was literature.

We walked for hours. The rubber plantations gave way to secondary forest, then to proper jungle. The light turned green and liquid. Sounds changed: the distant generator hum was gone, replaced by the whine of insects, the shriek of unseen birds, the constant, whispering drip of water. The world was no longer a document to be read; it was a wet, breathing creature.

My shirt stuck to my back. My father, impossibly, seemed to grow drier, more crisp, as if the adventure was starching him.

By midday, we reached a ridge. Below us, in a clearing on the next hill over, was the mission hospital.

It was not what I expected. Not a quaint, wooden chapel. It was a two-storey monstrosity of grey stone, built in a grim, utilitarian Gothic. It looked less like a beacon and more like a fortress that had surrendered. The roof was a skeleton of blackened timbers against the sky. Vines had scaled the walls, throttling the pointed-arch windows. One wing had entirely collapsed into a cairn of mossy stone.

“Magnificent,” Father breathed, his eyes alight. “Look at the degradation! The way the jungle is performing a literal critique of the architectural imposition!”

He practically scrambled down the slope, his scholarly fervour giving him a goat’s agility. I followed more slowly, a dread pooling in my stomach. The air here was still. Too still. The insect hum had dropped to a mere thrum. The place was an abscess in the forest.

We crossed the overgrown clearing. The main double doors were gone, leaving a dark maw. A smell drifted out: not just mildew and decay, but something chemical, medicinal, gone profoundly bad. The ghost of carbolic soap and despair.

Father paused on the threshold, not from fear, but from reverence. He removed his hat. “Listen,” he whispered.

I listened. I heard the drip of water inside. A faint, papery rustle. And beneath it, a low, rhythmic sound. Scrape… drag. Scrape… drag.

My blood turned to ice. It was the same sound from the fort.

Father heard it too, but his interpretation was different. “Rats, perhaps. Or a fallen branch moving in the wind.” He stepped inside.

I had no choice but to follow.

The interior was a cathedral of ruin. Shafts of greenish light pierced the broken roof, illuminating floating dust motes like suspended ash. The floor was a treacherous landscape of fallen plaster, broken tiles, and thick, velvety moss. Doorways led into blackness. A staircase climbed into shadow, missing half its banister.

Scrape… drag.

It came from our right, down a corridor.

Father was already moving toward it, taking a small flashlight from his satchel. The beam cut a wobbly path through the gloom. The corridor was lined with doors, some ajar, some closed. Plaque numbers hung askew: Ward 3. Surgery. Supply.

The sound was closer now. And there was another sound with it. A low, monotonous muttering.

We reached the end of the corridor. It opened into a large room—a ward. Iron bedframes, rusted into grotesque sculptures, were lined in rows. Most were empty. In the far corner, one bed was occupied.

A figure sat on the edge of it, bent over. It wore the tattered, stained remains of a nun’s habit. Its head was bowed, and in its hands, it held a roll of bandages. It was winding, and unwinding, winding and unwinding the same bandage around its own forearm. The action was precise, obsessive. Scrape (the bandage pulling tight), drag (a loose end brushing the floor).

The muttering was a list. “…spirit of camphor, tincture of mercury, laudanum twenty drops, chloride of lime solution, prayer, prayer, prayer…”

Father shone the light directly on her.

She did not startle. She slowly stopped her winding and looked up.

She had no face. Not in a grotesque, melted way. Where her face should have been was a smooth, blank oval of pale skin, like a nurse’s cap pulled too tight. No eyes, no nose, no mouth. Just that terrible, finished blankness.

Yet she saw us. I felt the attention like a pressure.

“Sister?” Father’s voice was a dry crackle. The anthropologist in him was battling the animal for control. “We mean no intrusion. We are here to document.”

The faceless head tilted. The muttering stopped. She stood up. She was tall, impossibly so. She dropped the bandage. It unspooled on the floor like a white tongue.

She took a step toward us. Then another. Her movement was not a shuffle, but a glide, as if on casters.

Father took a step back. The flashlight beam trembled on her blank visage.

“The treatment is not complete,” she said. Her voice was not a voice. It was the sound of many pages being turned at once in a quiet library. “The inventory is not balanced. You are a new entry. A new symptom.”

She raised a hand, long, pale fingers pointing at my father’s satchel. “Your papers. They require triage.”

“Stay back,” Father commanded, but the command had no air behind it.

She glided closer. The smell of bad medicine intensified, sweet and putrid.

I acted without thinking. I grabbed a piece of fallen plaster from the floor and hurled it. It didn’t hit her. It sailed through the space where she was and shattered against the far wall.

But she stopped. Her blank head turned toward the sound of the shattering. As if the disorder of it, the sudden break in the ritual, was an anomaly her programming couldn’t process.

“Run,” I hissed at my father.

He was paralyzed, staring at the faceless nun, his life’s work of analyzing ghosts colliding with the reality of one.

I grabbed his arm and yanked him backward. We stumbled into the corridor. I heard the soft shush of her habit behind us, the resumed mutter. “…contamination… quarantine procedure…”

We ran. We crashed through the main hall, slipping on moss, our footsteps echoing monstrously in the stone vault. We burst out into the blinding, green daylight and did not stop running until we were halfway up the opposite slope, lungs burning, thorns tearing at our clothes.

We collapsed behind a giant fig tree, gasping. Father’s hat was gone. His face was grey, his eyes wide with something that was not fear, but a profound, cognitive rupture.

“Did you see…” he panted. “The… the blankness… It wasn’t decay… It was… completion. A finished form.”

He was trying to fit it into his framework. A new category of ruin.

I just trembled, hugging my knees. I saw the bandage. I heard the list. The hospital wasn’t haunted by the dead. It was haunted by the procedure. The endless, mindless repetition of care that had failed. A stuck record of healing.

After a long time, he stood. He looked back down at the grey stone building, now silent and innocent-looking in its bower of vines. The scholar reasserted itself, a survival mechanism. “Fascinating. A phenomenological haunting. Not a person, but a process made manifest. Cheltham… imagine if his ministry… if bureaucracy itself…”

He was already writing the paper in his head. The terror was being metabolized into theory.

We walked back to Port James in a thick, exhausted silence. The journey took forever. The jungle now felt watchful, complicit.

We arrived at the bungalow as the last light was failing. Mother was on the veranda. She took one look at our torn clothes, our hollow eyes, and my father’s missing hat. She said nothing. She just went inside and put the kettle on.

In my room, I didn’t light the lamp. I sat in the dark. I opened my ration book to the last page I’d drawn—the doorway with the columns of accounting. I took my charcoal.

With a few swift, hard strokes, I drew the faceless nun. Not in detail, but in essence. A tall, blank column. And from her outstretched hand, I drew lines that were not bandages, but lists. Lists that wound around the trees, the stones, the house, my father’s satchel. A web of relentless, meaningless categorization.

This was the aftermath. Not just of war, or empire. Of intention. When a powerful intention dies, it doesn’t just vanish. It goes rogue. It repeats itself in the void, a phantom limb of purpose still trying to grasp, to organize, to treat a world that is no longer sick, but simply other.

I heard my father in his study. The scratch of his pen. He was already documenting the phenomenon. Making it his. Another entry in his ledger.

I closed the book. The house was quiet. Out in the night, I heard the distant, comforting clack-clack-clack of Joseph mending his nets. A sound of repair, of making whole. Not a list. A knot.

I knew then that Cheltham, with his clipboard, and the ledger-ghost, and the bandage-nun, and my father in his study, were all part of the same terrible continuum. The need to impose order on the beautiful, terrifying chaos of what simply is.

And I knew I had to find a different way to see. Before the world either got filed away, or treated into oblivion, or worse—before I started seeing blank faces in the mirror, and felt the urge to make lists of my own.

Chapter 4

CHAPTER 4

The first thing I stole the next morning was a sound.

It was the clack-clack-clack from the night before, but translated by dawn into something softer, more persistent: the whisper of palm fronds against the tin roof of the veranda. A sound of friction, not repair. I stole it with my ears, trying to hold its rhythm against the new, silent terror in my chest. The terror had crystallized overnight into a cold, sharp stone behind my ribs. A geological fact. I could not draw it out. I could only carry its weight.

Breakfast was a museum of normalcy. Mother placed a plate of papaya before me, its orange flesh a vulgar, cheerful sunrise. Father’s chair was empty. The scratch of his pen from the study was the real meal. He was in there with the door closed, writing the world into submission.

“He’s drafting a preliminary report for the Colonial Office,” Mother said, not looking at me. Her voice was carefully flat, a sheet ironed over a rumpled bed. “He believes it is a… localized psychic echo. A residue.”

“Of what?” My own voice was rough, unused.

She finally met my gaze. Her eyes were the colour of the sea under cloud, holding depths I had no line to sound. “Of treatment,” she said simply. Then she took my empty plate, though I had not eaten.

The stone in my chest grew heavier. A residue. He was already turning the nun, the ledger, the awful, repeating intention, into a footnote. Something to be catalogued under ‘P’ for ‘Phenomena, Aberrant.’ I pushed back from the table. The need to move, to act against the filing, was a physical pressure in my limbs.

Scene 1

My first challenge was the town itself.

Port James in the hard morning light was not the familiar, drowsy colonial outpost. It was a set of teeth, and I was a piece of gristle caught between them. Every whitewashed wall glared. Every orderly row of cabbages in the Administration gardens seemed a smug, green rebuke. I walked, aimless, my stolen sound of fronds replaced by the officious tap-tap of Cheltham’s boots on the packed earth ahead of me. He was moving with clipboard purpose from the Customs House to the telegraph office, a man weaving a net of mundane concerns.

I turned down towards the wharves, the one part of Port James that refused to be fully tamed. Here, the smell of salt and rotting fish and tar was a welcome assault. Here, things were tied down, but only temporarily. The challenge was to see it. Not as a colonial child, not as my father’s son, but as… something else. A thief of context.

I tried. I focused on a broken crate, its slats splayed like exhausted ribs. I did not see ‘debris.’ I saw the shape of the force that broke it—a careless drop from the sling of a winch. I saw the rusted nail protruding, not as ‘a hazard,’ but as a tiny, metal fang that had tasted timber and salt-air. It was exhausting. My mind kept slipping back into its old grooves: broken, dirty, need of repair. The world kept resolving into problems.

My second challenge was Joseph.

He was mending a net, as he always seemed to be, his fingers a blur of knowing motion. The net was a vast, wounded galaxy in his lap, and he was re-connecting its stars. I stood watching, a awkward satellite. He did not look up.

“You are standing in my light, Elias-boy.”

I shifted. The sun was behind me. It was not the light he meant.

“I saw something. In the jungle. Yesterday.”

His fingers paused for half a stitch. “Many things live in the jungle.”

“This wasn’t alive. It was… a memory. A bad one. Wearing a nun’s habit.”

He resumed his work. Pull, loop, tighten. “The Mission of the Sacred Heart left ten years ago. After the fever took the children. They closed the doors and did not look back.” Pull, loop, tighten. “Some doors, once closed, should not be opened.”

“This one is open. It’s walking around. It makes lists.”

His hands stilled completely. He looked at me then, and his eyes were old, older than the fort on the headland. They were the eyes of a man who has seen the tide come in and go out so many times he knows it is not the water that moves, but the land itself, sighing.

“A list is a small thing,” he said, but his voice was heavy. “A net is a list. Each knot a name for the empty space it holds. The problem is not the list. It is the fisherman who forgets the sea.”

It was the first true thing anyone had said to me since we returned. It did not explain the nun, but it named the dread that surrounded her. The forgetting. I squatted on my haunches, closer to him. The fishy, hemp smell of the net was solid, real.

“How do you not forget?” I asked, the question leaving me like a confession.

He held up a section of the net, a complex web of squares. “You must see the holes, Elias-boy. The things that are not there. The white man, he sees only the string. He sees the plan, the design, the thing he has made. He fills the holes with names—‘mackerel,’ ‘shark,’ ‘profit.’ But the hole itself? It is nothing. It is everything. It is what the water flows through. It is what the fish chooses. To mend a net, you must honour the hole. You must know its shape, or you will strangle the whole design.”

He went back to work. I stared at the net until my eyes lost focus, until what I saw was not the twine, but the negative spaces, the thousand little windows onto the bleached boards of the wharf behind. A lattice of absences. It was a new way of seeing. My first real theft of the day: the shape of a hole.

Scene 2

My third challenge was the ledger.

I found it in my father’s study after he left for the Administration Block, summoned by Cheltham. He had not locked the door. The arrogance of his order was its own security; who in this house would dare disturb the system?

The room smelled of him: of pipe tobacco, mildew, and the faint, acidic tang of intellectual possession. The ledger lay open on the blotter, next to his preliminary report. I did not touch the report. The ledger was the older scripture.

My father’s handwriting was a regiment of disciplined spikes and curves. The entries were not just numbers. They were judgements.

  • Specimen 447: Psittacula eupatria. Vivid green plumage degraded to greyish mould. Sign of atmospheric malaise. Noted 14th.
  • Phenomenon 23: Auditory echo in eastern gully. Repeats sound of rainfall on corrugated iron, though date is dry. Possibly a psychic impression of the ’37 floods. Filed under ‘Hauntings, Hydrological.’
  • Observation 88: Native fisherman ‘Joseph’ demonstrates atypical knot-tying technique. Efficiency rating: high. Cultural significance: negligible. Potential for adoption in standard practices?

I felt sick. This was his net. And he saw only the string. He was trying to name the holes, to fill them with his own definitions until nothing of the original, flowing mystery remained.

But then, near the bottom of the right-hand page, a new entry. Fresh ink.

  • Phenomenon Alpha (Provisional): Encountered 17th, deep jungle, grid reference Delta-Seven. Manifestation: humanoid, non-corporeal, attire consistent with extinct Missionary order. Behaviour: iterative, non-aggressive. Observed engaging in compulsive cataloguing behaviour (see attached sketch). Hypothesis: Not a ghost (see refutation of spiritualist theory, Appendix B), but a psychic scar. A fragment of concentrated colonial intention—the diagnostic/curative impulse of the Mission—left adrift post-abandonment. A purposeless purpose. Potential for contagion? Observation of subject Elias (juvenile, sensitive) recommended. Note: Subject’s propensity for unsanctioned sketching may provide useful data.

I stopped breathing. Subject Elias. I was no longer his son. I was a column in his ledger, a potential vector for ‘contagion.’ My sketches were ‘unsanctioned data.’ The stone in my chest turned to ice.

A floorboard creaked behind me.

I whirled, guilt scalding my throat. It was Mother. She stood in the doorway, holding a duster like a sceptre. She did not look surprised. She looked resigned.

“He will know you were in here,” she said quietly. “The angle of the ledger. The displacement of air. He notices.”

“He’s observing me. Like one of his damned birds.”

“Yes.” She stepped into the room, her eyes on the open page. She read the entry. Her face didn’t change, but a tiny muscle flickered in her jaw. “He is afraid, Elias. This,” she gestured at the ledger, the neat, terrible lines, “is what fear looks like on him. He is trying to pin the world down before it flies away and leaves him with nothing he understands.”

It was the most she had ever said about him. An alliance, offered in a whisper. I saw then that her quietness was not compliance. It was a deep, still water over a hidden reef. She had her own way of seeing.

“What do I do?” The question was a plea.

She moved to the bookshelf, not looking at me. “You learn to see what he cannot. You learn to be… un-file-able.” She ran a finger along a spine. “And you find someone who knows the texture of this particular fear. The Mission did not just leave. It was unmade. Some people here… they remember the unmaking.”

“Joseph?”

She shook her head. “Joseph knows the sea. This is a sickness of the land. Of brick and mortar and borrowed faith.” She finally turned to me. “Find Anya. The planter’s daughter. The Malcolms’ place, up the north track. She was a child at the Mission school. Before the fever. Before the… unmaking.”

Anya Malcolm. A name I knew only as a rumour. A girl who didn’t come to town, who was said to be ‘touched’ by the jungle. A story to frighten children. My father would have filed her under ‘Degeneracy, Suspected.’

My mother had just filed her under ‘Hope.’

Scene 3

The north track was a green tunnel, a vein into the island’s quieter heart. The challenge now was endurance. The sun was a hammer on the canopy, driving the humidity down into the air like nails. Every rustle in the undergrowth was a potential list-maker, a flutter of bandages. I saw the nun in every pale, slender tree trunk.

The Malcolm bungalow appeared suddenly, a relic of more optimistic times. It was less maintained than ours, the wood gone silvery-grey, the gardens a riot of orchids and bromeliads that had staged a successful coup against the imported roses. It didn’t look abandoned. It looked… assimilated.

She was on the veranda, shelling peas into a bowl. Anya. She was maybe sixteen, my age, but time seemed to have settled on her differently. She was thin, all sharp angles and watchful stillness, her hair the colour of burnt caramel, escaping its braid. Her eyes were the shock: a pale, clear grey, like quartz. They lifted to me as I approached, and there was no surprise in them. Only assessment.

“You’re the doctor’s boy,” she said. Her voice was low, unmusical. A fact-stating voice.

“Elias.”

“I know. You draw things that aren’t yours.” She dropped a handful of peas into the bowl. Click, click, click.

I halted at the bottom of the veranda steps. “How do you know that?”

“The air tells me. It’s thinner around you. You’ve made holes.” She looked past me, into the jungle. “You’ve seen the accountant, then.”

The name was so perfectly, terribly wrong. “The… accountant?”

“The one in the habit. That’s what I call her. She doesn’t pray. She tallies.” Anya’s quartz eyes fixed on me. “You saw her lists.”

It wasn’t a question. The relief of being believed, of being understood without explanation, was so profound it felt like a blow. I managed a nod.

“She’s getting closer to the houses,” Anya said, matter-of-fact. “For years she was only at the Mission ruins. Then the old clinic. Then the burial ground. Now she’s in the living jungle. She’s expanding her ledger.”

“What does she want?”

“What all accountants want. A balanced book. The Mission came to save souls. It failed. The books were left open. An imbalance. She is trying to close them. To find something of equal value to what was lost.” Anya set the bowl aside. “She will list everything. And when she is done listing, she will… rectify.”

“How do you know all this?”

For the first time, a flicker of something crossed her face. Not fear. Something older. Grief. “I was in the Mission school. The last one. I had the fever. I lived. The others… didn’t. I saw the sisters leave. They didn’t just close the door. They willed it shut. They poured all their failure, their guilt, their useless devotion into that act. You can’t pour that much intention into a place and expect it to just… soak away. It pools. It grows a shape.” She stood, brushing pea-dust from her skirt. “Come. I’ll show you what she’s really after.”

Scene 4

She led me not to the Mission ruins, but to a place behind the bungalow: a small, overgrown cemetery. Not the official colonial one, but a private plot. The Malcolm family graves. And one other, smaller, off to the side under a frangipani tree.

The stone was simple, almost crude. Lily Malcolm. Beloved Daughter. 1930-1937.

“My little sister,” Anya said. Her voice had gone flat, a sheet of tin. “The fever took her first. She was the initial entry in the ledger of that disaster.”

I stared at the grave. The frangipani blossoms had fallen on it, creamy and waxy like unused candles.

“The accountant comes here,” Anya whispered. “She stands over this spot. Not the other graves. Just this one. And her list… it grows longer here. I’ve seen it. The numbers in the air, like heat haze.”

I understood then, with a cold, sliding certainty. The nature of the antagonist. It wasn’t a ghost. It was a function. A colonial function—diagnosis, categorization, moral accounting—that had outlived its creators. Its power was the power of a mindless, relentless algorithm. It was auditing the island, trying to balance the spiritual ledgers left open by the Mission’s failure. And it had started with the first, irredeemable loss: a child in the ground.

It was looking for a credit to offset that devastating debit.

And my father, in his study, was creating a new ledger. Cheltham, with his clipboard, was another. They were all feeding it. Giving it new systems, new data. They were strengthening the very thing they sought to explain.

“We have to stop it,” I said, the words tasting like iron.

“How?” Anya asked, not with despair, but with a genuine, bleak curiosity. “You cannot reason with a receipt. You cannot plead with a column of figures. Its power is that it does not think. It only calculates.”

My minor defeat was not dramatic. It was the quiet shattering of a nascent hope. I had found an ally, one who saw the holes. But she saw no way to mend this net. The accountant’s function was too simple, too pure. It would list and list until everything—the trees, the stones, the sound of the sea, the memory of a little girl—was converted into a neutral, balanced value. A world rendered into a perfect, silent equation.

We walked back to the veranda in a silence thicker than the jungle’s. The late afternoon light was the colour of weak tea. As I turned to leave, defeated, Anya spoke again.

“There is one thing. One entry she cannot seem to make.”

I turned back. “What?”

“Herself.” Anya’s quartz eyes were almost luminous in the gloom. “She has no face. No name on her own list. She is the zero they forgot to carry. The blank space at the bottom of the page.” She hugged her arms around herself. “Maybe that’s the hole. The one she can’t fill. The one that could unravel her.”

It was a thread. A single, frail thread of possibility. But as I walked home, the stone of terror in my chest had a tiny, hairline crack in it. Not hope. Purpose. A thief’s purpose.

I would not try to destroy the accountant. Or explain her. Or file her away.

I would try to steal her face. To give her a name so true, so utterly un-file-able, that her own function would short-circuit. It was a mad, desperate idea. The kind of idea that comes from seeing the holes.

The veranda light was on at our bungalow. As I approached, I saw two figures silhouetted against it. My father. And Cheltham. They were standing close, speaking in low, urgent tones. Cheltham was handing my father something. A small, flat, rectangular box.

My father took it, nodded once, a sharp, military gesture. Then he turned and looked out into the dark, directly towards where I stood, frozen on the path. He couldn’t possibly see me. But he did not need to. He was observing the phenomenon of my absence.

He lifted the box. Moonlight glinted off its surface. Not a box. A case.

A case for specimen slides.

The hook that pulled me into the next chapter was not fear of the faceless nun. It was the sight of my father, already preparing a glass plate and a label, for me.

Chapter 5

CHAPTER 5

The plan was a theft. Not of a thing, but of a void. We would steal the absence of a face and replace it with a name so heavy it would break the scales. It was a child’s logic, the only kind I had ever trusted. If you cannot fight the monster, give it a birthday. Make it real. Make it owe the world something.

We met in the lee of the old lighthouse, a skeletal finger of rusted iron pointing at a sky it no longer commanded. Anya was a shadow against darker shadow, her stillness a different quality from the jungle’s. Leo was there, a nervous energy humming off him like heat from a wire. He had brought the tools of his own quiet rebellion: a bottle of ink stolen from the Residency’s supply closet, a sheaf of the thin, onionskin paper used for official triplicates, a nib pen worn to a forgiving softness.

“We are archivists of the un-recorded,” he said, his voice too loud in the salt-scoured silence. He was trying the words on, a suit of armour cut for a larger man.

“We are thieves,” I corrected, my voice low. “We are going to break into a filing system and leave a ghost.”

Anya said nothing. She was watching the sea chew on the headland. The lighthouse had been dark since the war. Another piece of infrastructure the retreating power had rendered useless, a tooth pulled so the gum would ache forever. Colonial aftermath: not just what they left behind, but the specific shape of the holes they left.

“The principle is sound,” Leo insisted, laying his papers on a flat stone. “Her entire function is to categorize, to assign value. To place. If we can create an entry for her that is fundamentally un-placeable—a paradox, a category error—it could disrupt the algorithm of her being.”

“She is not a machine, Leo.”

“Isn’t she?” He looked at me, his eyes wide behind his spectacles. “What is a colonial bureaucracy but a machine for making reality legible? For turning people into entries? She is the purest expression of that function. A saint of legibility.”

I thought of the nun in the dim church, her ledger open like a mouth. The way she had converted a screaming girl into a neutral value. Leo was right, and the truth of it was a cold stone in my gut. We were not fighting a person. We were fighting a principle. A ghost in the system.

“So we give her a name she cannot file,” I said. “A name that belongs to no list.”

“A true name,” Anya whispered. It was the first time she had spoken of magic without scorn. “Not the one they gave her. The one that grew in the dark.”

We bent over Leo’s papers. The plan was a fragile thing, spun from rumour and deduction. Sister Marguerite had come with the last contingent of the Grey Sisters, years after the main mission had been established. She was a late addition, a specialist. She kept to the old mission house deep in the overgrown rubber plantation, a place even the vines seemed to avoid. She emerged only for her audits. Her past was a column of blank spaces.

“We need a story,” I said. “Not a lie. A truth they missed. A receipt they forgot to collect.”

“We need an origin,” Anya said. “A first entry.”

I thought of my father, of the specimen case gleaming in the moonlight. The label he would prepare. Elias James O’Connor. Specimen: Colonial Adolescent, Degenerate Strain. I thought of the first thing I had ever stolen—that bruised piece of sky. Theft as the first act of self-definition.

“We start with a place,” I said. “A place that is gone.”

I took Leo’s pen. The ink was a blue so dark it was almost black, the colour of deep water. I did not write on his official paper. I turned over a sheet and used the back, the side meant for discard.

Subject: Elara. Place of Origin: The Drowned Village. (See Map Grid G-7, pre-typhoon survey. Now designated: Submerged Sector.)

I wrote it not as a nun’s history, but as a colonial report on a phenomenon. A report on a ghost. Leo leaned in, his breath whistling softly.

“Elara?”

“A name from a star chart in the old Governor’s library,” I murmured. “A moon that orbits a gas giant. A captive light. It felt… distant. Owned by nothing here.”

Village was re-settled post-1898 pacification campaigns. Population: approx. 300. Primary yield: rubber, obedience. Notable for sustained, low-grade resistance. Metric: consistent 5% quota deficit.

This was the language of the machine. We would feed it its own syntax.

Typhoon Maria, 1932. Village was not evacuated. Designated ‘non-viable’ by District Officer Cheltham. Loss of assets recorded; loss of life extrapolated from census data and filed under ‘Act of God/Asset Depreciation’.

My hand did not shake. The words were cold, clean. They were the words that had always been written about us. About this place. We were just changing the name on the form.

Post-event survey identified one survivor. Female child, approx. age 6. Found clinging to a kapok tree. No identification. Designated: Grey Sister Initiative, Candidate #7. Baptised: Marguerite. (Patron saint of lost causes.)

There it was. The conversion. From a child of a drowned village, a statistic of depreciation, to a numbered candidate, to a saint’s name. A perfect audit trail. We were not inventing. We were reconstructing the hole.

“The hole in her,” Anya said, her finger hovering over the word survivor. “It is not that she has no past. It is that her past was filed away. Made into a neutral fact. She is not missing a face. She is missing the grief.”

“So we give it back to her,” I said. “We make it un-ignoreable.”

Leo took the pen. His handwriting was a beautiful, precise copperplate, the handwriting of a born clerk. He began to transcribe my scrawled notes onto the pristine onionskin, creating the official document of a life that had been erased. He was not writing a story. He was creating a file. The most important file in the world. A ghost’s personnel record.

“We plant it,” Leo said, without looking up. “In the mission house. In the heart of her archive. It must be found, not given. Discovery is a form of truth.”

“And if she files it away?” Anya asked. “If she just… enters it?”

“Then we have given her a true name,” I said. “And she will have to live with it. Or die with it.”

The wind picked up, whistling through the lighthouse struts. It sounded like a low, constant note of inquiry. The sea below us was the colour of tarnished lead. We had our plan. A theft of absence. A gift of grief. It was the only kind of magic left in a world that had been fully accounted for.

The journey to the old mission was not a crossing of space, but a descent into a deeper stratum of silence.

We left before first light, when the world was a study in grey values. Anya led, moving through the jungle’s edge not like a trespasser, but like a thought passing through a mind. Leo and I followed, our footsteps clumsy echoes. We did not take the overgrown plantation road. That was a throat, and we would not walk into a mouth. We took the pig trails, the forgotten lines of resistance, paths made by creatures and people who did not wish to be seen.

The rubber trees were the worst. They stood in morbid, orderly lines, their trunks scarred with the diagonal slashes of old taps. The wounds had long since healed into thick, grey lips, but they gave the illusion that the trees were whispering, their mouths pressed shut against a scream. The air under the canopy was dead. It had been breathed too many times by the same desperate lungs. Colonial aftermath: a plantation is a field of captured labour, and the capture lingers in the sap-stink and the terrible geometry.

Leo’s nervous energy had condensed into a sweat on his temples. He clutched his satchel to his chest, the forged file inside it a sleeping coal. “It’s the silence,” he whispered. “It’s not an absence of sound. It’s a different kind of sound.”

He was right. It was the sound of consumption completed. The vines had not reclaimed this place because there was nothing left to reclaim. It had been sucked dry, its value extracted, its spirit tallied and shipped away. All that remained was the hollow shape.

By mid-morning, the plantation began to fray at its edges. The orderly lines broke into a snarled confusion of secondary growth, and then the jungle proper took hold again, louder and more vehement, as if asserting a victory. Here, the silence broke into a chorus of clicks and rustles and distant, alien calls. It was a relief. This was a living silence, full of private purposes. Not a corporate one.

Anya stopped us at a creek that cut through the limestone like a cold, black knife. “The mission spring,” she said. “Their water. Their purity.” The water was clear, but it had a mineral stillness to it that felt unwelcoming. On the far bank, almost swallowed by a curtain of creeping fig, was a low wall of fitted stone. A boundary.

We had reached the threshold of her world.

The mission house revealed itself not as a building, but as a slow, green smothering. It was a two-storey structure of whitewashed stone, or it had been. The jungle had laid claim with the soft, relentless force of a pillow pressed to a face. Vines sheathed the walls, burst through the empty window frames, and pulled at the corrugated iron roof until it sagged in a rusty sigh. The cross on the small chapel annex was askew, a liana wrapped around its base like a green fuse.

It was not ruins. Ruins are a conclusion. This was a suspension. It was a place that had been interrupted mid-function and left under a bell jar of green. The air was thick, sweet with the scent of rotting frangipani and wet stone.

“How do we get in?” Leo’s voice was a dry leaf rubbing against itself.

Anya pointed to a second-floor window where the vine cover was less dense. The remains of a wooden balcony hung beneath it, a rotten jaw. “The records will be upstairs. Away from the damp. Where the air is still.”

“The air is still everywhere here,” I said.

But she was already moving, finding fingerholds in the stone where the mortar had crumbled, using the very vines that were consuming the place as a ladder. She moved with a terrifying grace, a spider ascending its own web. Leo and I exchanged a look—a shared confession of our clumsiness, our profound unsuitability for this kind of theft—and began to follow.

The climb was a struggle against decay. Stone flaked away under my fingers. Wood pulped to the touch. Every hold was a betrayal. Halfway up, my foot slipped on a slick patch of moss and I swung out, my heart a frantic bird in my throat, before finding purchase again. Below me, Leo was frozen, his face pale, his arms trembling. He was not built for this physical kind of heresy. He was a forger of documents, a poisoner of files. I reached down, gripped his wrist, and hauled him up to the balcony’s edge. It groaned under our combined weight, a long, low sound of protest.

We tumbled through the window frame into the dark.

The room was a cathedral of dust.

Light fell in heavy, moted columns from holes in the roof, illuminating a landscape of shrouded shapes. Desks. Chairs. A skeletal loom. And shelves. Endless shelves, rising into the gloom, filled not with books, but with boxes. Uniform, grey cardboard boxes, each labelled with a neat, typed hand. I knew that hand. It was the same as in the ledgers.

Births/Deaths, 1910-1915. Land Grants, Sector D. Tithe Records, Parishes 1-4. Correspondence, Outgoing, Re: Native Uprisings.

This was not a library. It was a catacomb for facts. The air was cold and tasted of acid paper and slow, particulate death. My breath plumed before me. Leo stood beside me, hugging himself, his eyes wide behind their lenses as he read the labels. This was his territory, and it terrified him.

“It’s the back-up,” he whispered. “The physical archive. Everything they didn’t burn or take. The… the raw data.”

Anya was already moving down the central aisle, her feet leaving perfect prints in the dust. She was looking for something specific. I followed, my fingers trailing over the box edges. They were all so clean, so ordered, in this room of dissolution. A last, desperate stand against the entropy of the jungle. Against the entropy of memory.

We found her at the far end of the long room. Here, the boxes were different. Smaller. Metal. They were stacked on a large, steel table, and beside them sat the tools of her trade: a magnifying lamp, its bulb dead; a set of brass calipers; a stamp pad, the ink long dried to a cracked crust; and a neat row of nib pens, aligned with a military precision.

This was her desk. Her altar.

And on the wall behind it, pinned not with tape but with small, brass-headed tacks, was a map. A large-scale survey map of the island. It was covered in a transparent overlay, and on the overlay, in fine red ink, was a web of lines, nodes, and notations. It was a circuit diagram of control. A spider’s web of influence, debt, and consequence. I saw our bungalow marked. I saw the Residency. I saw the old fort. I saw the drowned village, a tiny ‘X’ in what was now blue-shaded sea. And I saw lines connecting them all, with numbers in tiny, perfect script beside each line. Percentages. Yields. Deficits.

It was her mind, made visible. Not a mind that thought in stories, but in vectors and values. It was the most horrifying thing I had ever seen.

Leo made a small, choked sound. He was staring at the centre of the table. There, lying separate from the metal boxes, was a ledger. It was open. The pages were blank.

“It’s new,” he said. “The next volume. She’s… preparing.”

For what? The question hung in the frozen air. For the final audit? For us?

A wave of doubt, cold and black as the creek water, washed over me. It was a tide that pulled at my knees, my stomach, my throat. Who were we? A colonial boy playing at rebellion with stolen charcoal. A clerk who loved the system he betrayed. A native girl who was herself a kind of living record of loss. We were fragments. Glitches.

And she was a principle. A logic. A function that converted screaming girls into balanced equations. We were coming to give a ghost a birthday. We were bringing a story to a machine that only processed facts. It was the most pathetic, arrogant kind of magic. The kind that gets you filed under ‘Specimen: Delusional’.

“This is madness,” I said, the words leaving me in a cloud of frozen breath. “She won’t care. She’ll just take the file and add it to a box. She’ll give it a reference number. We’re not giving her a crisis. We’re giving her… data.”

Anya turned from the map. Her face was grim. “You are thinking like a thief of things. You are not stealing a thing. You are performing a ritual. The paper is not the truth. The act is the truth. The act of placing it here.” She gestured at the sacred space of the desk. “In the heart of her temple. It is a violation of her order. It is a scream in her silence.”

“But what if she can’t hear screams?” Leo asked, his voice small. “What if her silence is… perfect?”

It was the core of the terror. Not that she was evil, but that she was finished. A closed system. A perfectly balanced equation where grief and joy cancelled each other out to a serene, neutral zero. We were trying to throw a stone into a well that had no bottom. There would be no splash. Only a long, diminishing sound of falling.

I looked at the blank ledger on the table. The promise of the next page. The endless, hungry next page that would consume everything—my father’s betrayal, Cheltham’s cold ambition, my own desperate thefts—and render it all into a balanced column. I saw my name there, in that future, perfect script. A final entry.

The doubt crystallized into a hard, certain fear. We would fail. And our failure would be so complete, so tidy, it would be as if we had never tried at all.

“We have to go,” I said. “Before she comes.”

“She is not here,” Anya said. “She is at the church. It is Tuesday. She audits the weekly tithes. We have time.”

“To do what? Leave our pathetic little ghost story on her desk and run?” The bitterness in my voice surprised me. It was the taste of the fear. “She’ll use it as a bookmark.”

Anya stepped close to me. In the dusty light, her eyes were not quartz. They were flint. “Then you do not believe in your own magic. The first magic. The theft.” She poked a finger hard against my chest. “You stole a piece of sky because you believed taking it meant it did not own you. That is the truth. Not the paper. The belief. You have to believe the name we give her is more real than the one she has. You have to believe it so completely that when she finds it, for one second, she believes it too. That is the short-circuit. That is the crack.”

She was right. I had built my whole life on that fragile, childish premise: that theft could be a kind of creation. That to take something was to define yourself against it. This was the same. We were not giving Sister Marguerite a history. We were taking her from the Grey Sisters. We were stealing her for the drowned village. It was an act of re-possession.

I looked at Leo. He was pale, but he nodded, a sharp, jerky motion. He believed in the file. In the perfect forgery. It was his act of theft.

“Where?” I asked.

Anya pointed to the centre of the steel table. “There. Where her hands rest. Where she cannot avoid it.”

Leo approached the table as if it were an altar indeed. He opened his satchel, withdrew the single sheet of onionskin. Our ghost’s birth certificate. He did not lay it flat. He stood it on its edge, leaning it against the blank ledger, so that it faced the chair. So that whoever sat down would be greeted by it.

Subject: Elara.

It looked absurdly small. A single sheet against the tonnes of filed history in this room. A whisper in a vault.

It was everything we had.

We left the way we came, descending the rotten balcony into the green gloom. The journey back should have felt like a retreat, but it didn’t. It felt like the long, held breath after a trigger has been pulled. The action was done. The theft was committed. The ghost was planted. Now there was only the waiting for the echo.

We did not speak until we broke from the rubber trees and saw the sea again, a vast, grey expanse of unanswered questions. The sky was the colour of a fresh bruise, deepening towards evening. I had a sudden, vivid memory of that first stolen piece of it. It had not saved me. But it had made me a thief. And that was the only self I had.

We stopped at the lighthouse, our starting point. It felt like a lifetime ago.

“What now?” Leo asked, brushing dust from his trousers with fastidious, useless strokes.

“Now we live our entries,” I said. “We wait for the audit.”

Anya was looking not at the sea, but inland, towards the town, towards the church. “It will not be long. She will find it tonight. Or tomorrow. The system will detect the anomaly.”

“And then?” Leo’s voice was thin.

“And then we see if a ghost can haunt a machine,” I said.

We parted there. Leo scurried back towards the Residency, a man returning to the belly of the beast. Anya melted into the twilight, a shadow returning to its source. I walked the path home alone, the stone of terror in my chest no longer cracked, but hollowed out. Emptied of doubt, filled with a cold, clear certainty. We had done it. For good or ill, we had introduced a paradox into a perfect system.

The veranda light of our bungalow was on again. A single, yellow eye in the gathering dark. As I approached, I saw only one silhouette this time. My father. He was sitting in his cane chair, not reading. Just sitting. Observing the phenomenon of the evening.

I climbed the steps. He did not look at me.

“You’re late,” he said. His voice was not a stone. It was a flat, measured instrument.

“I was walking.”

“In the plantation?”

The question lay between us, a baited trap. He knew. Or he suspected. Cheltham had spoken to him. The specimen case was waiting.

“Among other places,” I said.

He finally turned his head. In the lamplight, his face was all eroded cliffs and deep shadows. A map of a hard country. “There is talk,” he said. “At the Residency. Of instability. Of… corrupted records.”

I said nothing. I held very still, the way an animal does when the predator’s gaze passes over it.

“A system depends on the integrity of its records,” he continued, looking back into the dark. “Without that, it becomes… fiction. It becomes a story anyone can tell.” He took a slow sip from his glass. “I have always preferred facts, Elias. They are… containable. A story is a wild thing. It can turn on you.”

It was a warning. And a confession. He was a man of facts, of specimens under glass. He was afraid of the story we were trying to tell.

“I prefer stories,” I said, my voice quiet but clear in the still air. “They’re harder to file.”

He was silent for a long moment. Then he nodded, just once. Acknowledging a fact he could not contain. “Dinner is inside,” he said. The conversation was over. The audit, however, had just begun.

I went inside, the hook pulling me forward not into fear, but into a dreadful, exhilarating anticipation. We had thrown our stone. We had told our story to the silence.

Now we would wait for the silence to answer.

And in my room, on my desk, I saw it. A small, rectangular shape, placed with geometric precision in the very centre of the blotter. A specimen slide case. Empty. Its glass and label waiting.

It was not a threat. It was a promise. A promise of a final, perfect entry. My father had already chosen the category.

But as I lay in the dark later, listening to the sea, I thought of the other entry, the one leaning against a blank ledger in a green-smothered room. Elara. A name for a drowned moon. A story for a ghost.

Two files. Two futures. Two attempts to name what I was.

The system was preparing its label. And I, the thief, had prepared my own.

The collision would be the next chapter.

Chapter 6

CHAPTER 6

The silence answered with a key in a lock.

It was the sound of the Residency’s main door, three streets over, turning at precisely seven in the morning. I heard it from my window, a clean, oiled click that carried on the damp air. It was the sound of the system waking, stretching, and beginning its methodical day. My father had left an hour before, his footsteps on the gravel path a series of period marks ending the night’s uneasy sentence. The empty slide case still sat on my blotter, a rectangle of polished wood and brass that held a vacuum, a shaped absence waiting to be filled.

I did not touch it. To touch it would be to acknowledge its premise—that I was a specimen to be mounted. Instead, I stole the morning. A thin, watery light, the colour of weak tea, was trying to bleed through the cloud-wool. I took it with my eyes, memorising the way it failed to gild the edges of the fort on the headland. The fort was not a crumbling molar today. It was a keystone, holding back the weight of the grey sky. I filed the image away in the private ledger of my mind, under Resistance.

The hook in my chest did not pull me toward the Residency. It pulled me toward the green-smothered room, toward the ledger that held the ghost-name. But that was a story for shadows, for stolen hours. Daylight demanded a different kind of theft.

My greatest fear was not of capture, or of my father’s cold disappointment. It was of the label. The final, authoritative word that would pin me to the board, legs splayed, wings spread, forever explained. Elias Corvin: Colonial Subject, Category Unspecified. Aberration. The fear was a quiet one, a systemic one. It was the fear of being rendered into fact, of having my wild, stolen sky trimmed to fit a slide.

The life-or-death crisis, when it came, wore polished boots and carried a clipboard.

It was mid-morning. I was in the archives’ public annex, a vaulted room that smelled of dust and defeated ambition, transcribing shipping manifests from a decade past. The work was mindless, a penance. My father’s audit was a subtle pressure, a atmospheric change; the air grew denser, more scrutinising, around me.

Captain Rennick entered without sound. He was a tall man, whittled to a hard, efficient edge, the Residency’s chief of internal security. His uniform was his skin. He did not look at the shelves, or the high windows. He looked at the spaces between things, the places where secrets might pool.

“Elias Corvin.”

He said my name as if reading it from a list. Which he was. The clipboard held a single sheet, crisp and official.

“Captain.”

“Your work here is suspended. Effective immediately.”

The words were not loud, but in the hushed annex, they were a gunshot. The other two clerks did not look up, but their pens froze. They became part of the architecture.

“On whose authority?” My voice was calm. A flat sea.

“The audit.” He allowed a pause, a space for me to fill with panic. I left it empty. “A discrepancy has been identified. A procedural anomaly linked to your access logs.”

The stone we had thrown. It had not sunk into silence. It had been retrieved, examined for fingerprints.

“I see,” I said. It was all I could say. To ask for details would be to admit the possibility of guilt. To protest would be a confession.

“You are to come with me. For questioning.”

It was not a request. It was a relocation of a resource. I placed my pen down on the manifest, aligning it perfectly with the edge of the desk. A final, tidy act. As I stood, I stole a last look at the room—the dust motes swirling in a sunbeam that had finally broken through, the endless ranks of ledgers holding a million contained truths. I was being removed from the system of record. The first step to being re-catalogued.

He led me not to the interrogation cells in the basement—those were for obvious, messy crimes—but to a conference room on the second floor. A room of facts. A large, polished table reflected the grey sky like a dull mirror. On it lay a single file folder, fat and uncompromising. My father sat at the far end. He did not look at me. He was looking at the file as if it were a difficult specimen, its classification elusive.

“Sit, Elias,” Rennick said, gesturing to the chair in the centre of the table’s long side. A specimen placed on a slide. I sat.

The questioning was a surgical procedure. Rennick stood, pacing slowly behind me. My father remained still, a monument to objectivity.

“On the night of the 14th,” Rennick began, his voice a steady, probing instrument, “the access log for the Historical Anthropological Archive shows your keycode was used at twenty-three hundred hours.”

“I was in my room.”

“The log shows it.”

“Logs can be incorrect.”

“They are the system’s memory. We trust the system’s memory.” A pause. The squeak of his boot on waxed floor. “Unless it has been… persuaded to forget.”

I said nothing. I held my stolen sky inside me, a ragged piece of freedom pressed against my ribs.

“A sub-catalogue was accessed that night. Regional Folklore & Apocrypha. Volume Seven.” He stopped behind my chair. I could feel his presence like a cold spot. “Why would you need folklore, Elias?”

“I wouldn’t.”

“The log says you did.”

“Then the log is telling you a story.”

It slipped out. A piece of my private ledger, smuggled into the official record.

My father’s head lifted a fraction. His eyes met mine across the polished wood. In them, I saw not anger, but a profound professional disappointment. I had introduced a metaphor. I had contaminated the facts.

Rennick leaned forward, his hands on the back of my chair. “We have cross-referenced. The physical checkout ledger for that volume is missing its leaf for that entire week. A neat excision. Someone did not want a paper trail to match the digital one.” His voice dropped, conversational. “But excision is a fact in itself. It leaves a wound in the paper. A shape. We know the shape of what is gone.”

He moved to the table, opened the fat folder. He slid out a photograph. It was a page from the missing checkout ledger. A ghost-image. The page above had been pressed so hard that the pen strokes from the missing leaf had left an impression, a buried memory in the paper. He had used graphite rubbed lightly over the surface, bringing the impression to life like a spirit from a séance.

There, in faint, negative relief, was a book number. HA-FA-7. And below it, a name. The pressure had made it a deep, angry groove.

Elara.

The room ceased to breathe. The sound of the sea was gone. There was only the hum of the electric light and the terrible, perfect fact of that name on the table.

My father spoke, his voice hollow, eroded. “Who is Elara?”

It was the question I had asked the silence. Now the system was asking it of me. To answer would be to give them the ghost. To make her a fact for their folder. To pull her from the green room and pin her here, under this grey light.

“I don’t know,” I said. The lie was ash in my mouth.

“The name appears,” Rennick said, tapping the photograph, “in conjunction with a corrupted record. It appears now, in your possession. This is not coincidence. This is narrative.” He spat the word. “You are constructing a narrative, Elias. To obscure a truth. What are you trying to file away?”

I looked at my father. The man of facts. He was waiting. The empty slide case on my desk had been his question. This was mine. What are you? his silence asked. A thief of sky? Or a forger of records?

I was both. And in that moment, I understood the death that awaited me. It was not a physical one. It was the death of the thief, of the boy who stole skies. It was to be filed under Forger, under Saboteur. A contained, explainable fact. A story they could tell.

“I have nothing more to say,” I said.

It was my surrender. My symbolic death.

Rennick nodded, a crisp, satisfied motion. “Then we proceed to containment. You will be held pending a full disciplinary tribunal. Your access is revoked. Your movements are restricted to your residence, effective immediately.”

He gestured to the door. Two guards I hadn’t seen enter stood there. My father finally stood. He gathered the file folder, cradling the facts. He did not look at me again. I had become a problem to be processed. The audit was complete. The label was being prepared.

They walked me back through the Residency. Clerks and officials averted their eyes as I passed, a specimen being transported to the jar. The hook in my chest was gone. In its place was a cold, heavy weight. The stone we had thrown had been caught, and was now being used to anchor me to the seabed.

They delivered me to our house. My father gave the guards quiet instructions at the door. I was locked in. Not in my room, but in the entire, empty house. It was a larger specimen box.

I stood in the silent parlour. The house felt like a skull I was trapped inside. All my stolen things—the sketches of bruised skies, the memory of a green room, the sound of a ghost’s name—rattled around in the hollow space. I had failed. The story I was trying to tell had been declared fiction. Worse, it had been declared a fraudulent document.

The afternoon bled away. I did not sketch. I did not move. I watched the light die on the walls, a slow, grey suffocation. This was the defeat. This was the pin going through the thorax. I was named. I was known. I was contained.

As full dark fell, I found myself at the window of my father’s study. The slide case was gone from my desk. He had taken it back.
